L_{a}(U):=\frac{1}{2}\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}U\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{a}{2}\|U\|_{2}^{2}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}F(U)dx,\quad U\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}).

Set moreover the Pohozaev functional
P_{a}:H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

P_{a}(U):=\left(2^{*}_{s}\;\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}F(U)dx-\frac{a}{2}\|U\|_{2}^{2}}{\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}U\|_{2}^{2}}\right)_{+}^{\frac{1}{2s}},\quad U\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\;U\neq 0.

We further set

C_{po,a}:=\inf\big\{L_{a}(U)\mid U\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\setminus\{0\},\;P_{a}(U)=1\big\}

the Pohozaev minimum energy,
and

E_{a}:=\inf\big\{L_{a}(U)\mid U\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\setminus\{0\},\;L^{\prime}_{a}(U)=0\big\}

the least energy for
L_{a}
.

We recall the following result by Section
2.2
, where we further highlight the regularity, the positivity and the decay at infinity (see
[
79
, Theorems 1.1–1.3]
and
[
177
, Theorem 1.5]
).

Theorem 5.2.1
.

Assume
(f1)
with respect to
a>0
and
(f2)
.

•

There exists a positive minimizer for
C_{po,a}>0
, which is a weak solution of (
5.2.11
).

•

Every weak solution
U\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
of (
5.2.11
) is actually a strong solution, i.e.
U
satisfies (
5.2.11
) almost everywhere. Moreover

U\in H^{2s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap C^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{N})

for every
\sigma\in(0,2s)
.

•

Every weak solution
U\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
of (
5.2.11
) is strictly positive
and decays polynomially at infinity, that is there exist positive constants
C_{a}^{\prime},C_{a}^{\prime\prime}
such that

\frac{C_{a}^{\prime}}{1+|x|^{N+2s}}\leq U(x)\leq\frac{C_{a}^{\prime\prime}}{1+|x|^{N+2s}},\quad\textit{ for $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$}.

(5.2.12)

Observe that the bounding functions in (
5.2.12
) belong to
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
for any
q\in[1,+\infty]
.

•

If
(f3)
holds, then the Pohozaev identity

P_{a}(U)=1

holds for each nontrivial solution
U
of (
5.2.11
). As a consequence

E_{a}=C_{po,a}.

(5.2.13)

We observe that to reach the Pohozaev identity we need the solutions to be regular enough, fact that is given by
(f3)
.
The functional
P_{a}
will be of key importance for estimating
L_{a}
from below, see Lemma
5.2.3
and Lemma
5.2.6
.

We highlight the polynomial decay of solutions of (
5.2.11
). This decay is much less slower than the one, exponential, of the local case
s=1
. An alternative proof, which underlines some uniformity in
a
, can be found in Proposition
5.4.2
.

Remark 5.2.2
.

Since the equation is satisfied almost everywhere, we have also, by (
5.1.10
)

\displaystyle|(-\Delta)^{s}U(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|f(U(x))|+a|U(x)|\leq\beta|U(x)|+C_{\beta}|U(x)|^{p}+a|U(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{1}{1+|x|^{(N+2s)p}}+\frac{1}{1+|x|^{N+2s}}\right)\leq C\frac{1}{1+|x|^{N+2s}}

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

We end this Section by a technical lemma, which allows to link the level
L_{a}(u)
of a whatever function
u
, having a functional
P_{a}(u)\approx 1
, with the ground state
E_{a}
; in particular, it provides a useful lower bound for
L_{a}
.

Lemma 5.2.3
.

Let
u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and define

g(t):=\frac{1}{2s}\left(Nt^{N-2s}-(N-2s)t^{N}\right),\quad t\in\mathbb{R}.

(a)

If
P_{a}(u)\in\Big(0,\left(\frac{N}{N-2s}\right)^{\frac{1}{2s}}\Big)
, which we highlight is a neighborhood of
1
, then

L_{a}(u)\geq g(P_{a}(u))E_{a}.

(b)

If
u=U_{a}\left(\frac{\cdot-q}{t}\right)
for some
q\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, with
U_{a}
being a ground state of (
5.2.11
), then the above inequality is indeed an equality, that is

L_{a}\left(U_{a}\left(\frac{\cdot-q}{t}\right)\right)=g(t)E_{a}.

We highlight that the function
g
verifies

g(t)\leq 1\quad\hbox{ and }\quad g(t)=1\iff t=1.

Proof.

Let
\sigma:=P_{a}(u)
and set
v:=u(\sigma\cdot)
. Then
P_{a}(v)=1
. A straightforward computation shows, by using
P_{a}(v)=1
and
g(\sigma)>0
, that

L_{a}(u)=g(\sigma)L_{a}(v)\geq g(\sigma)C_{po,a}=g(\sigma)E_{a}.

We see that, if
u=U_{a}\left(\frac{\cdot-q}{t}\right)
, then by
P_{a}(U_{a})=1
we have
\sigma=P_{a}\left(U_{a}\left(\frac{\cdot-q}{t}\right)\right)=t
and thus
v=U_{a}(\cdot-q)
, which by translation invariance is again a ground state of (
5.2.11
); thus
L_{a}(v)=C_{po,a}
. This concludes the proof.

5.2.2

A family of equations: minimal radius map

In this Section we study equation (
5.2.11
) for variable values of
a>0
. Introduce the notation

\Omega[a,b]:=V^{-1}([m_{0}+a,m_{0}+b])\cap\Omega

and similarly
\Omega(a,b)
and mixed-brackets combinations.
We choose now a small
\nu_{0}>0
such that the minimum
m_{0}
is not heavily perturbed, namely

•

Berestycki-Lions type assumptions
(f1)
hold with respect to
a\in[m_{0},m_{0}+\nu_{0}]
– i.e., in particular,
F(t_{0})>\frac{1}{2}(m_{0}+\nu_{0})t_{0}^{2}
;

•

assumption
(V2)
holds with respect to
m_{0}+\nu_{0}
, i.e.
m_{0}+\nu_{0}<\inf_{\partial\Omega}V
;

•

\overline{\Omega[0,\nu_{0}]}\subset K_{d}\subset\Omega
for a sufficiently small
d>0
subsequently fixed, see Lemma
A.5
;

•

other conditions subsequently stated, see e.g. (
5.2.14
) and Lemma
5.2.6
.

We observe that, by construction, for
a\in[m_{0},m_{0}+\nu_{0}]
the considerations of Section
5.2.1
apply. Moreover, by scaling arguments on
C_{po,a}
, we notice that

a\in[m_{0},m_{0}+\nu_{0}]\mapsto E_{a}\in(0,+\infty)

is strictly increasing and that, up to choosing a smaller
\nu_{0}
, we have
1
1

1

Let
\nu_{0}
to be fixed, and
v
be a
m_{0}
-Pohozaev minimum (i.e.
L_{m_{0}}(v)=C_{po,m_{0}}
).
Let rescale
v
in such a way it belongs to the
(m_{0}+\nu_{0})
-Pohozaev set, i.e.
u:=v(\cdot/\theta)
for some explicit
\theta
:
computation shows
\theta>1
and
\theta\to 1
as
\nu_{0}\to 0
.
Thus
C_{po,m_{0}+\nu_{0}}\leq L_{m_{0}+\nu_{0}}(u)=\theta^{N-2s}L_{m_{0}}(v)=\theta^{N-2s}C_{po,m_{0}}
. By choosing
\nu_{0}
small we have
\theta^{N-2s}<2
.

E_{m_{0}+\nu_{0}}<2E_{m_{0}}
and thus we can find an
l_{0}=l_{0}(\nu_{0})\in\mathbb{R}
such that

E_{m_{0}+\nu_{0}}<l_{0}<2E_{m_{0}}.

(5.2.14)

As a final step in the proof of the main Theorem, we will make
\nu_{0}
and
l_{0}
moving such that
\nu_{0}^{n}\to 0
and
l_{0}^{n}\to E_{m_{0}}
.
We now define the set of
almost
ground states of (
5.2.11
)

S_{a}:=\left\{U\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\setminus\{0\}\mid L^{\prime}_{a}(U)=0,\;L_{a}(U)\leq l_{0},\;U(0)=\max_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}U\right\}\neq\emptyset.

We observe that we set the last condition in order to fix solutions in a point and prevent them to escape to infinity; the idea is to gain thickness and compactness (see
[
78
,
122
,
119
]
): notice indeed that, in the case of a proper ground state
U\in S_{a}
, then
U
is radially symmetric (see also (
5.5.81
)).
We further define

\widehat{S}:=\bigcup_{a\in[m_{0},m_{0}+\nu_{0}]}S_{a}.

The following properties of the set
\widehat{S}
will be of key importance in the whole paper.

Lemma 5.2.4
.

The following properties hold.

(a)

There exist positive constants
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}
such that, for each
U\in\widehat{S}
we have

\frac{C^{\prime}}{1+|x|^{N+2s}}\leq U(x)\leq\frac{C^{\prime\prime}}{1+|x|^{N+2s}},\quad\textit{ for $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$}.

(5.2.15)

(b)

\widehat{S}
is compact. Since it does not contain the zero function, we have

r^{*}:=\min_{U\in\widehat{S}}\|U\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}>0;

the maximum is attained as well.

(c)

We have

\lim_{R\to+\infty}\|U\|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}}=0,\quad\textit{uniformly for $U\in\widehat{S}$},

where the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}}
is defined in (
5.1.8
).
Moreover, if
(U_{n})_{n}\subset\widehat{S}
, and
(\theta_{n})_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
is bounded, then

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|U_{n}(\cdot+\theta_{n})\|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{n}}=0.

Proof.

We divide the proof in some steps.

Step 1.
We see that
\widehat{S}
is bounded. Indeed, by the Pohozaev identity, we have

\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}U\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{N}{s}L_{a}(U)\leq\frac{N}{s}l_{0}.

By (
1.2.7
) we have that also
\|U\|_{2^{*}_{s}}
is uniformly bounded.
Since
L^{\prime}_{a}(U)U=0
, we have by (
5.1.10
)

\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}U\|_{2}^{2}+a\|U\|_{2}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(U)Udx\leq\beta\|U\|_{2}^{2}+C_{\beta}\|U\|_{2^{*}_{s}}^{2^{*}_{s}}

which implies, by choosing
\beta<a
, that also
\|U\|_{2}^{2}
is bounded.

Step 2.
There exist uniform
C>0
and
\sigma\in(0,1)
such that

\|U\|_{\infty}\leq C,\quad[U]_{C^{0,\sigma}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C

(5.2.16)

for any
U\in\widehat{S}
. We postpone the proof of (
5.2.16
), as well as the proof of the uniform pointwise estimate (
5.2.15
) (where we use
(f3)
), since they will carry some arguments used in the proof of Theorem
5.1.4
in Section
5.4.1
; see Proposition
5.4.2
.

We show now
(b)
, which is a refinement of the fact that
S_{a}
itself is compact.

Step 3.
We observe first that
\widehat{S}
is closed. Indeed, if
U_{k}\in S_{a_{k}}\subset\widehat{S}
converges strongly to
U
, then up to a subsequence we have
a_{k}\to a\in[m_{0},m_{0}+\nu_{0}]
and, by the strong convergence, we have that the condition

E_{m_{0}}\leq L_{a}(U)\leq l_{0}

holds, which in particular implies that
U\not\equiv 0
.
Moreover, exploiting the weak convergence
U_{k}\rightharpoonup U
, and the almost everywhere convergence (together with the estimate on
f
, the uniform estimate (
5.2.15
) and the dominated convergence theorem), we obtain that for each
v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})

\displaystyle 0=L^{\prime}_{a_{k}}(U_{k})v=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\Delta)^{s/2}U_{k}(-\Delta)^{s/2}v\,dx+a_{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}U_{k}v\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(U_{k})v\,dx

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\Delta)^{s/2}U(-\Delta)^{s/2}v\,dx+a\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}Uv\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(U)v\,dx=L^{\prime}_{a}(U)v,

that is,
L^{\prime}_{a}(U)=0
. As regards the maximum in zero, we need a pointwise convergence. In order to get it, we exploit the fact that, by (
5.2.16
),
U_{k}
are uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and in
C^{0,\sigma}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and we apply Ascoli-Arzelà theorem to get local uniform convergence.
This shows that
U\in S_{a}\subset\widehat{S}
.

Step 4.
Let now
U_{k}\in S_{a_{k}}\subset\widehat{S}
.
By the boundedness, up to a subsequence we have
U_{k}\rightharpoonup U\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
a_{k}\to a\in[m_{0},m_{0}+\nu_{0}]
. We need to show that
\|U_{k}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\to\|U\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
; the closedness of
\widehat{S}
will conclude the proof.

As observed in Step 3, we have by the weak convergence that
L^{\prime}_{a_{k}}(U_{k})U_{k}=0=L^{\prime}_{a}(U)U
; hence, if
R>0
is some radius to be fixed, we gain

\displaystyle\left|\left(\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}U_{k}\|_{2}^{2}+a_{k}\|U_{k}\|_{2}^{2}\right)-\left(\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}U\|_{2}^{2}+a\|U\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Bigg|\int_{|x|\leq R}f(U_{k})U_{k}dx-\int_{|x|\leq R}f(U)Udx\Bigg|+

\displaystyle+\int_{|x|>R}|f(U_{k})U_{k}|dx+\int_{|x|>R}|f(U)U)|dx=:(I)+(II).

Fix now a small
\eta>0
. As regards
(II)
, we have by (
5.1.10
)

\int_{|x|>R}|f(U_{k})U_{k}|dx\leq\beta\int_{|x|>R}|U_{k}|^{2}dx+C_{\beta}\int_{|x|>R}|U_{k}|^{p+1}dx<\eta

for sufficiently (uniformly in
k
) large
R>0
thanks to (
5.2.15
); up to taking a larger
R
, it holds also for
U
.

Fixed this
R>0
, focusing on
(I)
, by Proposition
1.5.5
we have

\Bigg|\int_{|x|\leq R}f(U_{k})U_{k}dx-\int_{|x|\leq R}f(U)Udx\Bigg|<\eta

for sufficiently large
k=k(R)
.
Merging together, we obtain

\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}U_{k}\|_{2}^{2}+a_{k}\|U_{k}\|_{2}^{2}\to\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}U\|_{2}^{2}+a\|U\|_{2}^{2}

which with elementary passages leads to the claim.

Step 5.

Finally, we prove
(c)
. By contradiction, there exists an
\eta>0
such that, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
U_{n}\in\widehat{S}
which satisfies

\|U_{n}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{n}}>\eta.

By the compactness, we have, up to a subsequence,
U_{n}\to U\in\widehat{S}
as
n\to+\infty
.
Thus (notice that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|U(x)-U(\cdot)|^{2}}{|x-\cdot|^{N+2s}}dx\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and absolute integrability of the integral applies)

\eta<\|U_{n}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{n}}\leq\|U_{n}-U\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}+\|U\|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{n}}\to 0

which is an absurd.

For the second part, we argue similarly. Indeed, up to a subsequence,
U_{n}\to U
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
\theta_{n}\to\theta
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, thus

\displaystyle\|U_{n}(\cdot-\theta_{n})\|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|U_{n}-U\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}+\|\tau_{\theta_{n}}U-\tau_{\theta}U\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}+\|U(\cdot-\theta)\|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{n}}\to 0,

where
\tau_{\theta}
is the translation. This concludes the proof.

Remark 5.2.5
.

The compactness of the set
\widehat{S}
of (almost) ground states is somehow expected by thinking at the power case
f(u)=|u|^{p-2}u
: in this case, indeed, the ground state is unique (and nongenerate)
[
247
,
190
]
. On the other hand, in the general case (for examples for suitable sums of powers), uniqueness seems not to be the case
[
145
,
376
]
.

Gained compactness, we turn back considering the set of all the solutions (with no restrictions in zero), that is

\widehat{S}^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{N}}\tau_{p}(\widehat{S});

we observe that
\widehat{S}^{\prime}
is bounded. Moreover we define an open
r
-neighborhood of
\widehat{S}^{\prime}
, reminiscent of the perturbation approach in
[
184
,
13
,
146
]
,

S(r):=\{u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\mid d(u,\widehat{S}^{\prime})<r\}

that is

S(r)=\Big\{u=U(\cdot-p)+\varphi\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\mid U\in\widehat{S},\;p\in\mathbb{R}^{N},\;\varphi\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\;\|\varphi\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}<r\Big\}.

In order to re-gain some compactness, we aim to detect and somehow bound the point of translation and the size of the error. To this last goal, we
define a
minimal radius
map
\widehat{\rho}:H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
by

\widehat{\rho}(u):=\inf\left\{\|u-U(\cdot-y)\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\mid U\in\widehat{S},\;y\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\right\}.

We observe

u\in S(r)\implies\widehat{\rho}(u)<r,

(5.2.17)

and in addition

\widehat{\rho}(u)=\inf\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\mid u\in S(t)\},

where the infimum on the right-hand side is not attained. Finally,
\widehat{\rho}\in Lip(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\mathbb{R})
with Lipschitz constant equal to
1
, that is, for every
u,v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
,

|\widehat{\rho}(u)-\widehat{\rho}(v)|\leq\|u-v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

(5.2.18)

The detection of the point of translation will be instead more tricky, and will be investigated in Section
5.2.3
.

We end this Section with two technical lemmas. The first one is a direct consequence of Lemma
5.2.3
, and allows to link the level
L_{m_{0}}(u)
of a whatever function
u\in S(r)
with the ground state
E_{m_{0}}
, once
r
is sufficiently small; this further gives a lower bound for the functional
L_{m_{0}}
.

Lemma 5.2.6
.

Up to taking a smaller
\nu_{0}=\nu_{0}(l_{0})>0
,
there exists a sufficiently small
r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}(\nu_{0},r^{*})>0
such that, for every
u\in S(r^{\prime})
, we have

L_{m_{0}}(u)\geq g(P_{m_{0}}(u))E_{m_{0}}.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.2.3
(a), we know that the inequality holds if
P_{m_{0}}(u)
is in a neighborhood of the value
1
. Observe that
P_{a}(U)=1
if
U\in S_{a}
: by continuity and compactness,
P_{m_{0}}(U)\approx 1
if
U\in S_{a}
and
a\approx m_{0}
. In particular, by choosing a small value of
\nu_{0}
,
P_{m_{0}}(U)\approx 1
for
U\in\widehat{S}
. Indeed

P_{m_{0}}(U)=1+\frac{1}{2s}\frac{N}{N-2s}\;(a-m_{0})\frac{\|U\|_{2}^{2}}{\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}U\|_{2}^{2}}+o(1);

the addendum on the right-hand side can be bounded by the maximum and the minimum over
\widehat{S}
(notice that
(-\Delta)^{s/2}U
cannot be zero) and thus we can find a uniform small
\nu_{0}
.
Again by continuity we have that
P_{m_{0}}(u)\approx 1
for
u\in S(r^{\prime})
,
r^{\prime}
sufficiently small. Indeed

P_{m_{0}}(u)=1-\frac{1}{2s}\frac{N}{N-2s}\frac{1}{\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}U\|_{2}^{2}}\big(L_{m_{0}}(U(\cdot-p)+\varphi)-L_{m_{0}}(U(\cdot-p))\big)+o(1).

This concludes the proof.

We notice that the condition
E_{m_{0}+\nu_{0}}<l_{0}
keeps holding by decreasing
\nu_{0}
, so no ambiguity in the
l_{0}
-depending choice of
\nu_{0}
in Lemma
5.2.6
arises.
We focus now on the second lemma.

Lemma 5.2.7
.

There exist
\nu_{1}\in(0,\nu_{0})
and
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(\nu_{1})>0
such that

L_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}(U)\geq E_{m_{0}}+\delta_{0},\quad\textit{uniformly for $U\in\widehat{S}$}.

Proof.

Observe first that, since
\widehat{S}
is compact also in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, we have also finite and strictly positive minimum
\underline{M}
and maximum
\overline{M}
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{2}
. Consider
\nu_{1}\in(0,\nu_{0})
such that

E_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}-E_{m_{0}}>\frac{1}{2}(\nu_{0}-\nu_{1})\overline{M};

we notice that such
\nu_{1}
exists since, as
\nu_{1}\to\nu_{0}^{+}
, the left hand side positively increases while the right hand side goes to zero. Let now
a\in[m_{0},m_{0}+\nu_{0}]
; we consider two cases.
If
a\in[m_{0},m_{0}+\nu_{1}]
we argue as follow: for
U\in S_{a}
we have

\displaystyle L_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}(U)

\displaystyle=L_{a}(U)+\tfrac{1}{2}(m_{0}+\nu_{1}-a)\|U\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq E_{a}+\tfrac{1}{2}(m_{0}+\nu_{1}-a)\underline{M}=:(I)+(II);

now, the quantity
(I)
is minimum when
a=m_{0}
, while
(II)
is minimum when
a=m_{0}+\nu_{1}
; if both could apply at the same time, we would have as a minimum the quantity
E_{m_{0}}
. Since it is not possible, we obtain

\inf_{U\in\bigcup_{a\in[m_{0},m_{0}+\nu_{1}]}S_{a}}L_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}(U)>E_{m_{0}}.

If
a\in(m_{0}+\nu_{1},m_{0}+\nu_{0}]
instead, we have

L_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}(U)\geq E_{a}-\tfrac{1}{2}(a-(m_{0}+\nu_{1}))\overline{M}\geq E_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\nu_{0}-\nu_{1})\overline{M}

and thus, by the property on
\nu_{1}
,

\inf_{U\in\bigcup_{a\in[m_{0}+\nu_{1},m_{0}+\nu_{0}]}S_{a}}L_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}(U)\geq E_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\nu_{0}-\nu_{1})\overline{M}>E_{m_{0}}.

This concludes the proof, by taking as
\delta_{0}>0
the smallest of the two differences.

5.2.3

Fractional center of mass

As in
[
119
]
, inspired by
[
43
,
149
,
81
]
, we want to define a
barycentric map

\Upsilon
which, given a function
u=U(\cdot-p)+\varphi\in S(r)
, gives an estimate on the maximum point
p
of
U(\cdot-p)
; since
\varphi
is small and
U
decays (polynomially) at infinity,
p
is, in some ways, the
center of mass
of
u
. The idea will be to bound
\Upsilon(u)
in order to re-gain compactness.

Since the nonlocality comes into the very definition of the ambient space, we need the use of the norm (
5.1.8
), which we notice being stronger than the one induced by the Gagliardo seminorm.

Lemma 5.2.8
.

Let
r^{*}
be as in Lemma
5.2.4
. Then there exist a sufficiently large
R_{0}>0
, a sufficiently small radius
r_{0}\in(0,r^{*})
and a continuous map

\Upsilon:S(r_{0})\to\mathbb{R}^{N}

such that, for each
u=U(\cdot-p)+\varphi\in S(r_{0})
we have

|\Upsilon(u)-p|\leq 2R_{0}.

Moreover,
\Upsilon
is continuous and

-\Upsilon
is
shift-equivariant
, that is,
\Upsilon(u(\cdot+\xi))=\Upsilon(u)-\xi
for every
u\in S(r_{0})
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Proof.

Recalled that
r^{*}=\min_{U\in\widehat{S}}\|U\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}>0
, we have by Lemma
5.2.4

\|U\|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R_{0}}}<\tfrac{1}{8}r^{*},\quad\hbox{ uniformly for $U\in\widehat{S}$}

(5.2.19)

for
R_{0}\gg 0
. Thus

r^{*}\leq\|U\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|U\|_{B_{R_{0}}}+\|U\|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R_{0}}}<\|U\|_{B_{R_{0}}}+\tfrac{1}{8}r^{*}

which implies

\|U\|_{B_{R_{0}}}>\tfrac{7}{8}r^{*}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\|U\|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R_{0}}}<\tfrac{1}{8}r^{*}

for each
U\in\widehat{S}
. Consider now a cutoff function
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that

[0,\tfrac{1}{4}r^{*}]\prec\psi\prec[\tfrac{1}{2}r^{*},+\infty);

let
r_{0}\in(0,\frac{1}{8}r^{*})
and define, for each
u\in S(r_{0})
and
q\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, a
density
function

d(q,u):=\psi\left(\inf_{\tilde{U}\in\widehat{S}}\|u-\tilde{U}(\cdot-q)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}\right).

Notice that
d(\cdot,u)
is an integrable function: indeed
[u(\cdot+\xi)]_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}=[u]_{B_{R_{0}}(q+\xi)}
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and
q\mapsto\|u-\tilde{U}(\cdot-q)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}=\|\tau_{q}u-\tilde{U}\|_{B_{R_{0}}}
is continuous by

|\|\tau_{q}u-\tilde{U}\|_{B_{R_{0}}}-\|\tau_{p}u-\tilde{U}\|_{B_{R_{0}}}|\leq\|\tau_{q}u-\tau_{p}u\|_{B_{R_{0}}}\leq\|\tau_{q}u-\tau_{p}u\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\to 0

as
p\to q
, and the infimum over continuous functions is upper semicontinuous.

If we show that
d(\cdot,u)\geq 0
is not identically zero and it has compact support, then it will be well defined the quantity

\Upsilon(u):=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}q\,d(q,u)dq}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}d(q,u)dq}.

We show first that
d(\cdot,u)
has compact support. Indeed if
u=U(\cdot-p)+\varphi
and
\tilde{U}\in\widehat{S}
is arbitrary, then

\displaystyle\|u-\tilde{U}(\cdot-q)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}(\cdot-q)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}-\|U(\cdot-p)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}-\|\varphi\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}\|_{B_{R_{0}}}-\|U\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q-p)}-\|\varphi\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\geq\tfrac{6}{8}r^{*}-\|U\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q-p)};

take now
q\notin B_{2R_{0}}(p)
: if
x\in B_{R_{0}}(q-p)
, by the fact that
|x-(q-p)|<R_{0}
and
|q-p|\geq 2R_{0}
, we obtain that
|x|\geq R_{0}
, that is,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R_{0}}
. Therefore by (
5.2.19
)

\|u-\tilde{U}(\cdot-q)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}\geq\tfrac{6}{8}r^{*}-\|U\|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R_{0}}}\geq\tfrac{5}{8}r^{*}>\tfrac{1}{2}r^{*}

(5.2.20)

thus
\inf_{U\in\widehat{S}}\|u-\tilde{U}(\cdot-q)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}\geq\frac{1}{2}r^{*}
and hence
d(q,u)=0
for
q\notin B_{2R_{0}}(p)
; this means that
\mathop{\rm supp}(d(\cdot,u))\subset B_{2R_{0}}(p)
.

We show next that
d(\cdot,u)
is equal to
1
on a ball. Indeed if
u=U(\cdot-p)+\varphi

\displaystyle\inf_{\tilde{U}\in\widehat{S}}\|u-\tilde{U}(\cdot-q)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}\leq\|u-U(\cdot-q)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|U(\cdot-p)-U(\cdot-q)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}+\|\varphi\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}\leq\|\tau_{p-q}U-U\|_{B_{R_{0}}}+\tfrac{1}{8}r^{*}.

We can make the first term as small as we want by taking
|p-q|
small, that is

\inf_{U\in\widehat{S}}\|u-\tilde{U}(\cdot-q)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}\leq\tfrac{1}{4}r^{*}

for
q\in B_{r}(p)
,
r
small, which implies
d(q,u)=1
.

By the fact that
B_{r}(p)\subset\mathop{\rm supp}(d(\cdot,u))\subset B_{2R_{0}}(p)
we have the well posedness of
\Upsilon(u)
and

\Upsilon(u)=\frac{\int_{B_{2R_{0}}(p)}q\,d(q,u)dq}{\int_{B_{2R_{0}}(p)}d(q,u)dq}.

The main property comes straightforward, as well as the shift equivariance. We show now the continuity.
Indeed, assume
\|u-v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\frac{1}{8}r^{*}
. Then, by (
5.2.20
),

\|v-\tilde{U}(\cdot-q)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}\geq\|u-\tilde{U}(\cdot-q)\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}-\|v-u\|_{B_{R_{0}}(q)}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}r^{*}

and again we can conclude that
\mathop{\rm supp}(d(\cdot,v))\subset B_{2R_{0}}(p)
for each
\|u-v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\tfrac{1}{8}r^{*}
, where
p
depends only on
u
. Moreover, observe that
\int_{B_{2R_{0}}(p)}d(q,u)dq\geq\int_{B_{r}(p)}1\,dq\geq|B_{r}|=:C_{1}
not depending on
u
and
p
(and similarly
C_{2}:=|B_{2R_{0}}|
), and that
d(q,\cdot)
is Lipschitz
(since
\psi
and the norm are so, and the infimum over a family of Lipschitz functions is still Lipschitz).
Thus we have

\displaystyle|\Upsilon(u)-\Upsilon(v)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\int_{B_{2R_{0}}(p)}|q|\,|d(q,u)-d(q,v)|dq}{\int_{B_{2R_{0}}(p)}d(q,u)dq}+

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{B_{2R_{0}}(p)}|q|\,d(q,v)dq\frac{\int_{B_{2R_{0}}(p)}|d(q,v)-d(q,u)|dq}{\int_{B_{2R_{0}}(p)}d(q,u)dq\int_{B_{2R_{0}}(p)}d(q,v)dq}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B_{2R_{0}}(p)}|q|dq\frac{1}{C_{1}}\left(1+\frac{C_{2}}{C_{1}}\right)\|u-v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=:C_{p}\|u-v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

Since
C_{p}
can be bounded above by a constant of the type
C(1+\Upsilon(u))
, we have

\|u-v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq r_{0}\implies|\Upsilon(u)-\Upsilon(v)|\leq C(1+\Upsilon(u))\|u-v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})};

in particular this implies the continuity.

5.3

Singularly perturbed equation

We come back now to our equation

(-\Delta)^{s}u+V(\varepsilon x)u=f(u),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}.

(5.3.21)

It is known that the solutions of (
5.3.21
) can be characterized as critical points of the functional
I_{\varepsilon}:H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\to\mathbb{R}

I_{\varepsilon}(u):=\frac{1}{2}\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}u\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}V(\varepsilon x)u^{2}dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}F(u)dx,\quad u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})

where
I_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\mathbb{R})
, since
\|\cdot\|_{H_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
is a norm.

We start with a technical result. Let
\nu_{1}
be as in Lemma
5.2.7
; we want to show that the claim of the lemma
continues holding, for
\varepsilon
small, if we replace
L_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}
with
I_{\varepsilon}
, and
\widehat{S}
with
S(r_{0}^{\prime})\cap\{\varepsilon\Upsilon(u)\in\Omega[\nu_{1},\nu_{0}]\}
,
r_{0}^{\prime}
small.

Lemma 5.3.1
.

Let
\nu_{1}
and
\delta_{0}
be as in Lemma
5.2.7
.
Then there exist
\delta_{1}\in(0,\delta_{0})
and
r_{0}^{\prime}=r_{0}^{\prime}(\delta_{1})\in(0,r_{0})
sufficiently small, such that for every
\varepsilon
small we have

I_{\varepsilon}(u)\geq E_{m_{0}}+\delta_{1}

for each
u\in\{u\in S(r_{0}^{\prime})\mid\varepsilon\Upsilon(u)\in\Omega[\nu_{1},+\infty)\}\supset\{u\in S(r_{0}^{\prime})\mid\varepsilon\Upsilon(u)\in\Omega[\nu_{1},\nu_{0}]\}
.

Proof.

First we improve Lemma
5.2.7
for
L_{a}
in the direction of the nonautonomous equation. Indeed, by the assumption, we have
V(\varepsilon\Upsilon(u))\geq m_{0}+\nu_{1}
, that is

L_{V(\varepsilon\Upsilon(u))}(U)\geq L_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}(U)\geq E_{m_{0}}+\delta_{0}

for any
U\in\widehat{S}
. Moreover, if
u=\tilde{U}(\cdot-p)+\tilde{\varphi}\in S(r_{0})
then, by Lemma
5.2.8
,
\varepsilon p\in\Omega_{2\varepsilon R_{0}}\subset\Omega_{2R_{0}}
which is compact.
By uniform continuity of
V
and boundedness from above of
\widehat{S}
, we have

L_{V(\varepsilon p)}(U)\geq E_{m_{0}}+\delta_{0}/2

(5.3.22)

for all
U\in\widehat{S}
and
\varepsilon
small enough.

Let now
r_{0}^{\prime}
to be fixed and
u=U(\cdot-p)+\varphi\in S(r_{1})
. Then we have

I_{\varepsilon}(u)=I_{\varepsilon}(U(\cdot-p)+\varphi)=I_{\varepsilon}(U(\cdot-p))+I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(v)\varphi

for some
v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
in the segment
[U(\cdot-p),u]
. Notice that
v
lies in a ball of radius
\max\widehat{S}+r_{0}^{\prime}
and
I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
sends bounded sets in bounded sets (uniformly on
\varepsilon
); thus there exists a constant
C
, not depending on
U
,
p
and
\varphi
, such that

I_{\varepsilon}(u)\geq I_{\varepsilon}(U(\cdot-p))-C\|\varphi\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\geq I_{\varepsilon}(U(\cdot-p))-\delta_{1}/2

(5.3.23)

for
\|\varphi\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}<r_{0}^{\prime}
sufficiently small.
Recalled that
\varepsilon p\in\Omega_{2R_{0}}
we have, by the uniform continuity of
V
and the uniform estimate (
5.2.15
), for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
,

I_{\varepsilon}(U(\cdot-p))\geq L_{V(\varepsilon p)}(U)-\delta_{1}/2

and the claim comes from (
5.3.23
) and (
5.3.22
), since, for
\delta_{1}<\delta_{0}/4
,

I_{\varepsilon}(u)\geq E_{m_{0}}+\delta_{0}/2-\delta_{1}\geq E_{m_{0}}+\delta_{1}.

Before introducing the penalized functional, we state another technical lemma, which gives a (trivial, but useful) lower bound for
I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(v)v
for small values of
v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

Lemma 5.3.2
.

There exists
r_{1}>0
sufficiently small and a constant
C>0
such that

I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(v)v\geq C\|v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2}

(5.3.24)

for every
\varepsilon>0
and
v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
with
\|v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq r_{1}
.

Proof.

We have, by (
5.1.10
) with
\beta<\frac{1}{2}\underline{V}
,

\displaystyle I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(v)v

\displaystyle\geq\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}v\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\underline{V}v^{2}dx-\beta\|v\|_{2}^{2}-C_{\beta}\|v\|_{p+1}^{p+1}

\displaystyle\geq\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}v\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\underline{V}\|v\|_{2}^{2}-C_{\beta}\|v\|_{p+1}^{p+1}

(5.3.25)

\displaystyle\geq C\|v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2}-C_{\beta}\|v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{p+1}\geq C^{\prime}\|v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2}

where the last inequality holds for
\|v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
small, since
p+1>2
.

Remark 5.3.3
.

For a later use, we observe that one can improve (
5.3.25
) by

\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}v\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\underline{V}\|v\|_{2}^{2}-2^{p}C_{\beta}\|v\|_{p+1}^{p+1}\geq C\|v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2}

(5.3.26)

up to choosing a smaller
r_{1}
.

5.3.1

A mass-concentrating penalization

We want to study now a penalized functional (see
[
83
,
78
,
119
]
), that is
I_{\varepsilon}
plus a term which forces solutions to stay in
\Omega
.

Since
V>m_{0}
on
\partial\Omega
, we can find an annulus around
\partial\Omega
where this relation keeps holding, that is

V(x)>m_{0},\quad\hbox{ for $x\in\overline{\Omega_{2h_{0}}\setminus\Omega}$}

for
h_{0}
sufficiently small. We then define the
mass-concentrating
penalization functional
Q_{\varepsilon}:H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\to\mathbb{R}

Q_{\varepsilon}(u):=\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\alpha}}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}-1\right)_{+}^{\frac{p+1}{2}},\quad u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})

where
\alpha\in(0,\min\{1/2,s\})
.

We observe that, for every
u,v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
,

Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u)v=\frac{(p+1)}{\varepsilon^{\alpha}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\alpha}}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}-1\right)_{+}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon)}uv\,dx

and it is straightforward to prove the following estimate

Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u)u\geq(p+1)Q_{\varepsilon}(u).

(5.3.27)

We thus set

J_{\varepsilon}:=I_{\varepsilon}+Q_{\varepsilon}

the
penalized
functional. It results that
Q_{\varepsilon}
and
J_{\varepsilon}
are in
C^{1}(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\mathbb{R})
.

We want to find critical points of
J_{\varepsilon}
and show, afterwards, that these critical points, under suitable assumptions, are critical points of
I_{\varepsilon}
too, since
Q_{\varepsilon}
will be identically zero.
Let
\varepsilon=1
: observed that
Q_{1}(u)
vanishes if
u
have much mass inside
\Omega
, we see that
J_{1}(u)=I_{1}(u)
holds when the mass of
u

concentrates
in
\Omega
; this is why we say that
Q_{\varepsilon}

forces

u
to stay in
\Omega
. Similarly, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, much less mass must be found outside
\Omega/\varepsilon
.

We start by two technical lemmas. The first one gives a sufficient condition to pass from weak to strong convergent sequences in a Hilbert space, similarly to the convergence of the norms.

Lemma 5.3.4
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and let
(u_{j})_{j}\subset H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
be such that

\|J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}\to 0\quad\textit{ as $j\to+\infty$}.

(5.3.28)

Assume moreover that
u_{j}\rightharpoonup u_{0}
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
as
j\to+\infty
, and that

\lim_{R,j\to+\infty}\|u_{j}\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R})}=0

(5.3.29)

for
q=2
and
q=p+1
.
Then
u_{j}\to u_{0}
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
as
j\to+\infty
.

Proof.

We have by the weak lower semicontinuity of the norm

\liminf_{j\to+\infty}\|u_{j}\|_{H_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\geq\|u_{0}\|_{H_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

(5.3.30)

Moreover

\displaystyle\|u_{j}\|_{H_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(u_{j})u_{j}dx+I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})u_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(u_{j})u_{j}dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(u_{0})u_{0}dx\Bigg)+\Big(I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})u_{j}-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{0})u_{0}\Big)+

\displaystyle+I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{0})u_{0}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(u_{0})u_{0}dx=:(I)+(II)+\|u_{0}\|_{H_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2};

if we prove that

\limsup_{j\to+\infty}\big((I)+(II)\big)\leq 0

we are done, because together with (
5.3.30
) we obtain

\|u_{j}\|_{H_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\to\|u_{0}\|_{H_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\quad\hbox{ as $j\to+\infty$},

which implies the claim, since
H^{s}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is a Hilbert space.

Focus on
(I)
; we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(f(u_{j})u_{j}-f(u_{0})u_{0})dx=\int_{B_{R}}(f(u_{j})u_{j}-f(u_{0})u_{0})dx+

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}}(f(u_{j})u_{j}-f(u_{0})u_{0})dx=:(I_{1})+(I_{2}).

The piece
(I_{2})
can be made small for
j
and
R
sufficiently large, by exploiting the estimates on
f
, assumption (
5.3.29
) and the absolute continuity of the Lebesgue integral for
u_{0}
. For such large
R
and
j
, up to taking a larger
j
, we can make the piece
(I_{1})
small by Proposition
1.5.5
.

Focus now on
(II)
; we first observe that by exploiting Hölder inequalities and again classical arguments we have
I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})u_{0}\to I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{0})u_{0}
.
Thus we have, by (
5.3.28
),

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to+\infty}\left(I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})u_{j}-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{0})u_{0}\right)=-\liminf_{j\to+\infty}\left(Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})u_{j}-Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})u_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\liminf_{j\to+\infty}\Bigg(\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\alpha}}\|u_{j}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}-1\right)_{+}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon)}u_{j}^{2}dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon)}u_{j}u_{0}dx\Bigg)\Bigg)\leq 0

where the last inequality is due to the following fact: observe first that
u_{j}\rightharpoonup u_{0}
in
H^{s}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
thus (by restriction)
u_{j}\rightharpoonup u_{0}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon))
; by definition of weak convergence and by the lower semicontinuity of the norm, we have

\liminf_{j\to+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon)}u_{j}^{2}dx\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon)}u_{0}^{2}dx=\lim_{j\to+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon)}u_{j}u_{0}dx,

that is

\liminf_{j\to+\infty}\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon)}u_{j}^{2}dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon)}u_{j}u_{0}dx\Bigg)\geq 0.

Noticed that
a_{n}\geq 0
and
\liminf_{n}b_{n}\geq 0
imply
\liminf_{n}(a_{n}b_{n})\geq 0
, we conclude.

The second Lemma is a lower bound for
J_{\varepsilon}
with respect to the functional
L_{m_{0}}
.
We highlight that in what follows we understand that the case
m_{0}=\underline{V}
, i.e.
m_{0}

global
minimum, gives rise to a not-perturbed result.

Lemma 5.3.5
.

Set
C_{min}:=\frac{1}{2}(m_{0}-\underline{V})\geq 0
we have, for
\varepsilon
small and
u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
,

J_{\varepsilon}(u)\geq L_{m_{0}}(u)-C_{min}\varepsilon^{\alpha}.

Proof.

We have, recalling that
m_{0}
is the infimum of
V
over
\Omega_{2h_{0}}
and
\underline{V}
is the infimum over
\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

\displaystyle J_{\varepsilon}(u)

\displaystyle=L_{m_{0}}(u)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(V(\varepsilon x)-m_{0})u^{2}dx+Q_{\varepsilon}(u)

\displaystyle\geq L_{m_{0}}(u)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon)}(V(\varepsilon x)-m_{0})u^{2}dx+Q_{\varepsilon}(u)

\displaystyle\geq L_{m_{0}}(u)-C_{min}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}^{2}+Q_{\varepsilon}(u).

If
\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}^{2}\leq 2\varepsilon^{\alpha}
we have the claim by the positivity of
Q_{\varepsilon}(u)
.
If instead
\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}^{2}\geq 2\varepsilon^{\alpha}
, then

Q_{\varepsilon}(u)\geq\left(\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{\alpha}}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}\right)^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{\alpha}}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}.

Thus

J_{\varepsilon}(u)\geq L_{m_{0}}+\left(\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{\alpha}}-C_{min}\right)\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}^{2}\geq L_{m_{0}}

for
\varepsilon
small. This concludes the proof.

5.3.2

Critical points and truncated Palais-Smale condition

In order to get critical points of
J_{\varepsilon}
we want to implement a deformation argument. As usual, we need a uniform estimate from below of
\|J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u)\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}
, and this is the next goal.

First, by the strict monotonicity of
E_{a}
, let us fix
l_{0}^{\prime}=l_{0}^{\prime}(\nu_{1})>0
such that

E_{m_{0}}<l_{0}^{\prime}<E_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}};

as well as
\nu_{0}
and
l_{0}
, even
l_{0}^{\prime}
will be let vary as
(l_{0}^{\prime})^{n}\to E_{m_{0}}
in the proof of the existence.

Lemma 5.3.6
.

Let
r_{0}
and
r_{1}
be as in Lemma
5.2.8
and Lemma
5.3.2
. There exists
r_{2}^{\prime}\in(0,\min\{r_{0},r_{1}\})
sufficiently small with the following property: let
0<\rho_{1}<\rho_{0}\leq r_{2}^{\prime}
and
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\subset S(r_{2}^{\prime})
be such that

\displaystyle\quad\|J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}\to 0\quad\textit{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$},

(5.3.31)

\displaystyle J_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\leq l_{0}^{\prime}<E_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}},\quad\textit{ for any $\varepsilon>0$},

(5.3.32)

with the additional assumption

(\widehat{\rho}(u_{\varepsilon}))_{\varepsilon}\subset[0,\rho_{0}],\quad(\varepsilon\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon}))_{\varepsilon}\subset\Omega[0,\nu_{0}].

Then, for
\varepsilon
small

\widehat{\rho}(u_{\varepsilon})\in[0,\rho_{1}],\quad\varepsilon\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon})\in\Omega[0,\nu_{1}].

We notice, by (
5.3.31
) and (
5.3.32
), that
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
resembles a particular (truncated) Palais-Smale sequence. As an immediate consequence of the Lemma, set the sublevel

J_{\varepsilon}^{c}:=\{u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\mid J_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq c\}

we have the following theorem.

Theorem 5.3.7
.

There exists
r_{2}^{\prime}\in(0,\min\{r_{0},r_{1}\})
sufficiently small with the following property: if
0<\rho_{1}<\rho_{0}\leq r_{2}^{\prime}
, then there exists a
\delta_{2}=\delta_{2}(\rho_{0},\rho_{1})>0
such that, for
\varepsilon
small

\|J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u)\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}\geq\delta_{2}

for any

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\in\left\{u\in S(r_{2}^{\prime})\cap J_{\varepsilon}^{l_{0}^{\prime}}\mid(\widehat{\rho}(u),\varepsilon\Upsilon(u))\in\big((0,\rho_{0}]\times\Omega[0,\nu_{0}]\big)\setminus\big([0,\rho_{1}]\times\Omega[0,\nu_{1}]\big)\right\}

\displaystyle\supset\left\{u\in S(r_{2}^{\prime})\cap J_{\varepsilon}^{l_{0}^{\prime}}\mid\rho_{1}<\widehat{\rho}(u)\leq\rho_{0},\;\varepsilon\Upsilon(u)\in\Omega(\nu_{1},\nu_{0}]\right\}.

Remark 5.3.8
.

Arguing as in the last part of the proof of Lemma
5.2.4
, noticed that
\widehat{S}
is compact not only in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
but also in
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
for
q\in[2,2^{*}_{s}]
, if
(U_{n})_{n}\subset\widehat{S}
and
(\theta_{n})_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
is included in a compact, we have

\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{n}(\cdot+\theta_{n})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{n}}=0,

where the norm
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{n}}
is defined in (
5.1.9
).

Proof of Lemma
5.3.6
.

We use the notation, for
h>0
,

\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{h}:=(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})/\varepsilon=(\Omega/\varepsilon)_{h}

and notice that if
h<h^{\prime}
then
\Omega/\varepsilon\subset\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{h}\subset\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{h^{\prime}}
.
Let
r_{2}^{\prime}<\min\{r_{0},r_{1}\}
to be fixed.

Step 1.

An estimate for
u_{\varepsilon}
.

We have, for
u_{\varepsilon}=U_{\varepsilon}(\cdot-p_{\varepsilon})+\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in S(r_{2}^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega/\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{\varepsilon}(\cdot-p_{\varepsilon})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega/\varepsilon)}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\varphi_{\varepsilon}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega/\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{\varepsilon}(\cdot-p_{\varepsilon}+\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon}))\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega/\varepsilon-\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon}))}+C\|\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{\varepsilon}(\cdot-p_{\varepsilon}+\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon}))\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega/\varepsilon-\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon}))}+Cr_{2}^{\prime}.

By the fact that
\varepsilon\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon})\in\Omega[\nu_{1},\nu_{0}]\subset\Omega
, we have that
0\in\Omega/\varepsilon-\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon})
and thus
\Omega/\varepsilon-\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon})
expands in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Moreover by Lemma
5.2.8
we have
\theta_{\varepsilon}:=\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon})-p_{\varepsilon}\in B_{2R_{0}}
compact. By Remark
5.3.8
, for
\varepsilon
small we have

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega/\varepsilon)}\leq(1+C)r_{2}^{\prime}=C^{\prime}r_{2}^{\prime}.

(5.3.33)

Let

n_{\varepsilon}:=\left[\frac{\sqrt{1+4h_{0}/\varepsilon}+1}{2}\right]\in\mathbb{N}

which by definition satisfies
\varepsilon n_{\varepsilon}(n_{\varepsilon}+1)\leq h_{0}
and
n_{\varepsilon}\to+\infty
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. We have

\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\varepsilon}}\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}(i+1)}^{\varepsilon}\setminus\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}i}^{\varepsilon})}^{2}\leq\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}(n_{\varepsilon}+1)}^{\varepsilon}\setminus\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon})}^{2}\leq(C^{\prime}r_{2}^{\prime})^{2}

and similarly

\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\varepsilon}}[u_{\varepsilon}]_{\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}(i+1)}^{\varepsilon}\setminus\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}i}^{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R}^{N}}^{2}\leq(C^{\prime}r_{2}^{\prime})^{2},\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\varepsilon}}\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p+1}(\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}(i+1)}^{\varepsilon}\setminus\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}i}^{\varepsilon})}^{p+1}\leq(C^{\prime}r_{2}^{\prime})^{p+1}

thus, for some
C=C(r_{2}^{\prime})
,

\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\varepsilon}}\left(\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}(i+1)}^{\varepsilon}\setminus\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}i}^{\varepsilon}}^{2}+\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p+1}(\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}(i+1)}^{\varepsilon}\setminus\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}i}^{\varepsilon})}^{p+1}\right)\leq C.

This implies that there exists
i_{\varepsilon}\in\{1,\dots,n_{\varepsilon}\}
such that

\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{A^{\varepsilon}}^{2}+\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p+1}(A^{\varepsilon})}^{p+1}\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}}\to 0\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$},

(5.3.34)

where

A^{\varepsilon}:=\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}(i_{\varepsilon}+1)}^{\varepsilon}\setminus\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}i_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}

and
C
depends on
r_{2}^{\prime}
(we will omit this dependence).

Step 2.

Split the sequence.

Consider cutoff functions
\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{N})

\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}i_{\varepsilon}}\prec\varphi_{\varepsilon}\prec\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}(i_{\varepsilon}+1)}

such that
\|\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}}=o(1)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
(which is possible because the distance between
\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}i_{\varepsilon}}
and
\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}(i_{\varepsilon}+1)}
is
n_{\varepsilon}\to+\infty
).

Define

u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}:=\varphi_{\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon},\quad u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}:=(1-\varphi_{\varepsilon})u_{\varepsilon}\quad\hbox{ and }\;u_{\varepsilon}=u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}+u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)};

notice that both
\mathop{\rm supp}\big(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\big)
and
\mathop{\rm supp}(F(u_{\varepsilon})-F(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})-F(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}))
are contained in
A^{\varepsilon}
, that is where we gained the estimate of the norm. Moreover, since

\mathop{\rm supp}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\subset\Omega_{n_{\varepsilon}(i_{\varepsilon}+1)}^{\varepsilon}\subset\Omega_{\varepsilon n_{\varepsilon}(n_{\varepsilon}+1)}/\varepsilon\subset\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon

we have, by definition of
Q_{\varepsilon}
, that
Q_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})=0
,
Q_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=Q_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})
and

Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})=0,\quad Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}).

(5.3.35)

Step 3.

Relations of the functionals.

We show that

|I_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})-I_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})-I_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})|\to 0\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}

from which

J_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=I_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})+I_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})+Q_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})+o(1).

(5.3.36)

Indeed

\displaystyle|I_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})-I_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})-I_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})|\leq

\displaystyle\;\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\Delta)^{s/2}u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(-\Delta)^{s/2}u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\Bigg|+\int_{A^{\varepsilon}}|V(\varepsilon x)u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}|

\displaystyle+\int_{A^{\varepsilon}}|F(u_{\varepsilon})-F(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})-F(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})|

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\,(I)+(II)+(III).

The second piece can be easily estimated by the boundedness of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
and
V
, and the information on the
L^{2}
-norm given by (
5.3.34
), i.e
(II)\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}}
.
Similarly, as regards
(III)
, we estimate each single piece separately, in the same way: use (
5.1.10
) and the information on the
L^{2}
-norm and the
L^{p+1}
-norm given by (
5.3.34
), obtaining
(III)\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}}
.

Focus instead on (I). Recall that
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\subset S(r_{2}^{\prime})
, and thus
\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{2}
is bounded. We have

\displaystyle(I)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2N}}\frac{|u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(y)||u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(y)|}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq 2C\int_{\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}i_{\varepsilon}}\times\complement(\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}(i_{\varepsilon}+1)})}\frac{|u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(y)||u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(y)|}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy+

\displaystyle\quad+2C\int_{A^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(y)||u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(y)|}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy

\displaystyle=:2C\big((I_{1})+(I_{2})\big)

since on
\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}i_{\varepsilon}}\times\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}i_{\varepsilon}}
and
\complement(\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}(i_{\varepsilon}+1)})\times\complement(\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}(i_{\varepsilon}+1)})
the integrand is null. Focusing on
(I_{1})

\displaystyle(I_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{(\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}i_{\varepsilon}}\times\complement(\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}(i_{\varepsilon}+1)}))\cap\{|x-y|>n_{\varepsilon}\}}\frac{|u_{\varepsilon}(x)u_{\varepsilon}(y)|}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}i_{\varepsilon}}\times\complement(\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}(i_{\varepsilon}+1)}))\cap\{|x-y|>n_{\varepsilon}\}}\frac{u^{2}_{\varepsilon}(x)+u^{2}_{\varepsilon}(y)}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}i_{\varepsilon}}}u^{2}_{\varepsilon}(x)\int_{\complement(\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}(i_{\varepsilon}+1)})\cap\{|x-y|>n_{\varepsilon}\}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dy\,dx+

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\complement(\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}(i_{\varepsilon}+1)})}u^{2}_{\varepsilon}(y)\int_{\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}i_{\varepsilon}}\cap\{|x-y|>n_{\varepsilon}\}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq C\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{2}^{2}\int_{|x-y|>n_{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy\leq\frac{C}{{n_{\varepsilon}}^{2s}}\to 0\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

Focusing on
(I_{2})
we have

\displaystyle(I_{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{A^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}\Big(|(\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y))u_{\varepsilon}(x)|+|\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)(u_{\varepsilon}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}(y))|\Big)\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\Big(|(\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x))u_{\varepsilon}(x)|+|(1-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y))(u_{\varepsilon}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}(y))|\Big)dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{A^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{2}|u_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy+\int_{A^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|u_{\varepsilon}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy+

\displaystyle+2\int_{A^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)||u_{\varepsilon}(x)||u_{\varepsilon}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}(y)|}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{A^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{2}|u_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}+\int_{A^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|u_{\varepsilon}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}+

\displaystyle+2\Bigg(\int_{A^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{2}|u_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigg(\int_{A^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|u_{\varepsilon}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle A^{2}+B^{2}+2AB

and we see that both
A
and
B
go to zero:
B=[u_{\varepsilon}]_{A^{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R}^{N}}\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}}
by (
5.3.34
), while for
A
we exploit that
\|\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\to 0
.
Indeed, let
\alpha_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{1}{\|\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}}
; we have

\displaystyle A^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{A^{\varepsilon}}|u_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{2}\int_{|x-y|\leq\alpha_{\varepsilon}}\frac{|\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dy\,dx+

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{A^{\varepsilon}}|u_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{2}\int_{|x-y|>\alpha_{\varepsilon}}\frac{|\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dy\,dx

\displaystyle\leq C\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(A^{\varepsilon})}^{2}\Bigg(\|\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2}\int_{|z|\leq\alpha_{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{|z|^{N+2s-2}}dz+4\|\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2}\int_{|z|>\alpha_{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{|z|^{N+2s}}dz\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}}\alpha_{\varepsilon}^{-2s}\left(\|\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2}\alpha_{\varepsilon}^{2}+1\right)=\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}}\|\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2s}\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}^{2s+1}}\to 0\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

Thus
(I_{2})\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}^{2s+1}}+\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}^{s+1}}+\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{n_{\varepsilon}}\to 0
,
which reaches the claim.

Step 4.

Relations of the derivatives.

We have

\|I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}\to 0\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$},

(5.3.37)

from which, joined to (
5.3.35
),

J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})+I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})+Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})+o(1).

(5.3.38)

Indeed by Hölder inequality, for any
v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
,

|I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})v-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})v-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})v|\leq\int_{A^{\varepsilon}}|f(u_{\varepsilon})-f(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})-f(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})||v|dx

and again we argue in the same way as in the third piece of Step 3, observing that, by (
5.1.10
),
|f(u_{\varepsilon})||v|\leq\beta|u_{\varepsilon}||v|+C_{\beta}|u_{\varepsilon}|^{p}|v|
thus

\displaystyle\int_{A^{\varepsilon}}|f(u_{\varepsilon})||v|dx

\displaystyle\leq\beta\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(A^{\varepsilon})}\|v\|_{2}+C_{\beta}\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p+1}(A^{\varepsilon})}^{p}\|v\|_{p+1}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\beta\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(A^{\varepsilon})}+C_{\beta}\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p+1}(A^{\varepsilon})}^{p}\right)\|v\|_{H^{s}}

and hence the claim. In particular,
|\big(I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})\big)u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}|\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}}
.
We see also that

I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}=o(1).

(5.3.39)

Indeed

\displaystyle|I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}|\leq\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\Delta)^{s/2}u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(-\Delta)^{s/2}u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}dx\Bigg|+

\displaystyle+\int_{A^{\varepsilon}}|V(\varepsilon x)u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}dx|+\int_{A^{\varepsilon}}|f(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}dx|=:(I)+(II)+(III)

where for
(I)
and
(II)
we argue as in Step 3 obtaining
(I)+(II)\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}^{2s}}+\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}}
, while for
(III)
we argue as in (
5.3.38
) obtaining
(III)\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}}
.

Step 5.

Convergence of
u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}
.

Observing that the support of
u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}
is outside
\Omega/\varepsilon
, we have with arguments similar to Step 3 that, by (
5.3.33
),

\|u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq r_{1}.

(5.3.40)

Indeed, focusing only on the nonlocal part, we have (recall that
\mathop{\rm supp}\big(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\big)\subset\complement(\Omega/\varepsilon)
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2N}}\frac{|u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{\complement(\Omega/\varepsilon)\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{\complement(\Omega/\varepsilon)\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{2}|u_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy+

\displaystyle\quad+4\int_{\complement(\Omega/\varepsilon)\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|u_{\varepsilon}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy

and we use again the final argument in Step 3 and (
5.3.33
) to gain, for
\varepsilon
small,

\|u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2}\leq(C+o(1))\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\complement(\Omega/\varepsilon))}^{2}+C[u_{\varepsilon}]_{\complement(\Omega/\varepsilon),\mathbb{R}^{N}}^{2}\leq(Cr_{2}^{\prime})^{2}

where
C
does not depend on
r_{2}^{\prime}
. We choose thus
r_{2}^{\prime}
such that (
5.3.40
) holds.

This allows us to use Lemma
5.3.2
.
By joining (
5.3.31
), (
5.3.38
), (
5.3.39
), (
5.3.24
), (
5.3.27
) we obtain

\displaystyle o(1)

\displaystyle=J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}=I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}+I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}+Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}+o(1)

\displaystyle\geq C\|u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2}+(p+1)Q_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})+o(1)

or more precisely (we highlight this for a later use), for some
C=C(r_{2}^{\prime})
,

o(1)=J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\geq C\|u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2}+(p+1)Q_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})-\left(\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}}+\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}^{2s}}\right),

(5.3.41)

which implies (since
Q_{\varepsilon}
is positive) that

\|u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\to 0\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}

(5.3.42)

and

Q_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})\to 0\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

(5.3.43)

As a further consequence, (
5.3.42
) and the boundedness of
V
imply

I_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})\to 0\quad\hbox{and}\quad I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})\to 0\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

(5.3.44)

Step 6.

Convergence of
I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})
.

In particular we obtain from (
5.3.44
), together with (
5.3.36
) and (
5.3.43
), that

J_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=I_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})+o(1).

(5.3.45)

We want now to show that

I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\to 0\quad\hbox{in $(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}$}\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

(5.3.46)

Start observing that (
5.3.44
) together with (
5.3.37
) give

I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})+o(1);

(5.3.47)

let now
v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and evaluate
I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})v
. We want to exploit (
5.3.47
) together again with the assumption (
5.3.31
). In order to do this we need to pass from
u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}
to
u_{\varepsilon}
, but getting rid of
Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})
on which we have no information. Thus we introduce a cutoff function
\tilde{\varphi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
such that

\Omega_{\frac{3}{2}h_{0}}\prec\tilde{\varphi}\prec\Omega_{2h_{0}}

and hence

\mathop{\rm supp}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\subset\Omega_{h_{0}}/\varepsilon\subset\Omega_{\frac{3}{2}h_{0}}/\varepsilon\subset\{\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon\cdot)\equiv 1\}\subset\mathop{\rm supp}(\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon\cdot))\subset\Omega_{2h_{0}}/\varepsilon.

Thus we have

\displaystyle I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})v

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})(\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon\cdot)v)+(1+\|v\|_{2})o(1)

\displaystyle=I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})(\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon\cdot)v)-\left(I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})(\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon\cdot)v)-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})(\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon\cdot)v)\right)+(1+\|v\|_{2})o(1)

\displaystyle=J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})(\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon\cdot)v)-\left(I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\right)(\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon\cdot)v)+(1+\|v\|_{2})o(1).

Indeed, we justify
(*)
as done in Step 3: notice that
u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\equiv 0
outside
\Omega_{h_{0}}/\varepsilon
and
1-\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon\cdot)\equiv 0
in
\Omega_{\frac{3}{2}h_{0}}/\varepsilon
, so in the annulus
(\Omega_{\frac{3}{2}h_{0}}/\varepsilon)\setminus(\Omega_{h_{0}}/\varepsilon)
both
u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
and
1-\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon\cdot)
are zero; notice also that
\complement(\Omega_{\frac{3}{2}h_{0}}/\varepsilon)
and
\Omega_{h_{0}}/\varepsilon
get far one from the other as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Thus we have

\displaystyle|I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})((1-\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon\cdot))v)|

\displaystyle=2\int_{(\Omega_{h_{0}}/\varepsilon)\times\complement(\Omega_{\frac{3}{2}h_{0}}/\varepsilon)}\frac{|u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(x)||(1-\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon y))v(y)|}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{(\Omega_{h_{0}}/\varepsilon)\times\complement(\Omega_{\frac{3}{2}h_{0}}/\varepsilon)}\frac{|u_{\varepsilon}(x)||v(y)|}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy\leq(1+\|v\|_{2})o(1)

where in the last passage we argue as for
(I_{1})
in Step 3. Notice that
o(1)
does not depend on
v
.
Thus we obtain

\displaystyle|I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})v|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\|J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}+\|I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}\right)\|\tilde{\varphi}(\varepsilon\cdot)v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}

\displaystyle+(1+\|v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})})o(1)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\|J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}+\|I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}\right)\|v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\big(C+o(1)\big)+

\displaystyle+(1+\|v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})})o(1)

where in the last inequality we argue as in Step 5 (again
o(1)
does not depend on
v
).
Concluding, we have, by choosing
\|v\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=1
, that

\displaystyle\|I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}+\|I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})-I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}\right)\big(1+o(1)\big)+o(1)\to 0

by using (
5.3.31
) and (
5.3.47
).

Step 7.

Weak convergence of
u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
.

Set
q_{\varepsilon}:=\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon})
to avoid cumbersome notation. Since
(\varepsilon q_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\subset\Omega[0,\nu_{0}]\subset\Omega
bounded in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we have that up to a subsequence

\varepsilon q_{\varepsilon}\to p_{0}\in\overline{\Omega[0,\nu_{0}]}\subset K_{d}\subset\Omega.

Moreover, by estimating the norm of
u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
with the norm of
u_{\varepsilon}
(as done before, in Step 5, for
u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}
), where
u_{\varepsilon}
belongs to
S(r_{2}^{\prime})
bounded in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, we have that also
u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
is a bounded sequence, and thus is so
u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(\cdot+q_{\varepsilon})
, which implies, up to a subsequence

u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(\cdot+q_{\varepsilon})\rightharpoonup\tilde{U}\;\;\hbox{in $H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$}\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

For each
v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})

we apply this weak convergence to the following equalities, derived from (
5.3.46
),

\displaystyle o(1)

\displaystyle=I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})v(\cdot-q_{\varepsilon})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\Delta)^{s/2}u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(y+q_{\varepsilon})(-\Delta)^{s/2}v(y)dy+

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}V(\varepsilon y+\varepsilon q_{\varepsilon})u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(y+q_{\varepsilon})v(y)dy-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(y+q_{\varepsilon}))v(y)dy

\displaystyle=:(I)+(II)+(III).

For
(I)
and
(III)
we obtain by the weak convergence and by Proposition
1.5.5

(I)\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\Delta)^{s/2}\tilde{U}\,(-\Delta)^{s/2}v\,dy\quad\hbox{ and }\quad(III)\to-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(\tilde{U})v\,dy\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

For
(II)
instead we have

\displaystyle\left|{(II)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}V(p_{0})\tilde{U}v}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\,\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(V(\varepsilon y+\varepsilon q_{\varepsilon})-V(p_{0}))^{2}v^{2}(y)\Bigg)^{1/2}\|u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(\cdot+q_{\varepsilon})\|_{2}+

\displaystyle+V(p_{0})\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(y+q_{\varepsilon})v(y)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\tilde{U}v\Bigg|

where the first term goes to zero (thanks to the boundedness of
u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
) by the dominated convergence theorem, while the second thanks to the weak convergence. Thus we finally obtain

L^{\prime}_{V(p_{0})}(\tilde{U})v=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\Delta)^{s/2}\tilde{U}\,(-\Delta)^{s/2}v\,dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}V(p_{0})\tilde{U}v\,dy-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(\tilde{U})v\,dy=0

for each
v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, that is

L^{\prime}_{V(p_{0})}(\tilde{U})=0.

(5.3.48)

Step 8.

Strong convergence of
u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
.

We want to show the strong convergence of
u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(\cdot+q_{\varepsilon})
, that is

u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(\cdot+q_{\varepsilon})\to\tilde{U}\quad\hbox{ in $H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$}\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

(5.3.49)

Set
\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}:=u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(\cdot+q_{\varepsilon})-\tilde{U}\rightharpoonup 0
, again by (
5.3.46
) we have

\displaystyle o(1)

\displaystyle=I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot-q_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=L^{\prime}_{V(p_{0})}(\tilde{U})\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}+\left(\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}V(\varepsilon y+\varepsilon q_{\varepsilon})\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}^{2}dy\right)+

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(V(\varepsilon y+\varepsilon q_{\varepsilon})-V(p_{0}))\tilde{U}\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(f(\tilde{U})-f(\tilde{U}+\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}))\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}dy

\displaystyle=:\Bigg(\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}V(\varepsilon y+\varepsilon q_{\varepsilon})\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}^{2}dy\Bigg)+(I)

\displaystyle\geq\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}\|_{2}^{2}+\underline{V}\|\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}\|_{2}^{2}+(I)

where we have used (
5.3.48
). We obtain by the boundedness of
V
and (
5.1.10
)

\displaystyle(I)\geq

\displaystyle-2\|V\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}|\tilde{U}||\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}|dy-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\left(2\beta|\tilde{U|}+C_{\beta}(2^{p}+1)|\tilde{U}|^{p}\right)|\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}|dy-

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\left(\beta|\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+2^{p}C_{\beta}|\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}|^{p+1}\right)dy=o(1)-\beta\|\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}\|_{2}^{2}-2^{p}C_{\beta}\|\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}\|_{p+1}^{p+1};

in the last passage we have used that
\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup 0
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, thus by Remark
1.4.2

|\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}|\rightharpoonup 0
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and hence in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and in
L^{p+1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
(observing that
\tilde{U}^{p}\in L^{1+\frac{1}{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
).
Merging together all the things we have, by (
5.3.26
) and choosing
\beta<\frac{1}{2}\underline{V}
,

o(1)\geq\|(-\Delta)^{s/2}\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}\|_{2}^{2}+(\underline{V}-\beta)\|\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}\|_{2}^{2}-2^{p}C_{\beta}\|\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}\|_{p+1}^{p+1}\geq C\|\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2}

and thus
\tilde{w}_{\varepsilon}\to 0
strongly in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, that is the claim.

Step 9.

Localization.

Observe first that
\tilde{U}\not\equiv 0
. Indeed, if not, by (
5.3.42
), (
5.3.49
) and translation invariance of the norm we would have

\displaystyle r^{*}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\|U_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\|U_{\varepsilon}(\cdot-p_{\varepsilon})+\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}+\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\|\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Big(\|u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}+\|u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\Big)+r_{2}^{\prime}\leq r_{2}^{\prime}<r^{*},

impossible.
By (
5.3.49
) we obtain also

I_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\to L_{V(p_{0})}(\tilde{U})\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

Thus we find, by using also (
5.3.45
) and (
5.3.32
),

L_{V(p_{0})}(\tilde{U})=I_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})+o(1)=J_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})+o(1)\leq l_{0}^{\prime}+o(1)

and hence, letting
\varepsilon\to 0
,

L_{V(p_{0})}(\tilde{U})\leq l_{0}^{\prime}<E_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}.

(5.3.50)

Moreover by (
5.3.48
) and
\tilde{U}\not\equiv 0
, we have

E_{V(p_{0})}\leq L_{V(p_{0})}(\tilde{U});

joining together the two previous inequalities we find
E_{V(p_{0})}<E_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}
which implies, by the monotonicity of
E_{a}
, that

V(p_{0})<m_{0}+\nu_{1}.

Joining this information to the fact that
p_{0}\in\Omega
(and in particular
V(p_{0})\geq m_{0}
) we have
p_{0}\in\Omega[0,\nu_{1})
, that is

\varepsilon\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon})\to p_{0}\in\Omega[0,\nu_{1})\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

(5.3.51)

Exploiting again (
5.3.50
) (observe that
E_{m_{0}+\nu_{1}}<E_{m_{0}+\nu_{0}}<l_{0}
) together with
L^{\prime}_{V(p_{0})}(\tilde{U})=0
, and
\tilde{U}\neq 0
, we have that
\tilde{U}
belongs to
S_{V(p_{0})}
up to translations, that is

U:=\tilde{U}(\cdot-y_{0})\in S_{V(p_{0})}\subset\widehat{S}

for some suitable
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. So, set

p_{\varepsilon}:=q_{\varepsilon}+y_{0}

we have

\|u_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}-U(\cdot-p_{\varepsilon})\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\to 0\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

(5.3.52)

For a later use observe also that

\varepsilon p_{\varepsilon}\to p_{0}\in\Omega[0,\nu_{1})\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

(5.3.53)

Step 10.

Conclusions.

By (
5.3.51
) we have that

\varepsilon\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon})\in\Omega[0,\nu_{1})

definitely for
\varepsilon
small. This is the first part of the claim.
Moreover, by (
5.3.52
) and (
5.3.42
) we gain

\|u_{\varepsilon}-U(\cdot-p_{\varepsilon})\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\to 0\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}

(5.3.54)

and thus, since
\widehat{\rho}(u_{\varepsilon})\leq\|u_{\varepsilon}-U(\cdot-p_{\varepsilon})\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
by definition, also
\widehat{\rho}(u_{\varepsilon})\to 0
and hence

\widehat{\rho}(u_{\varepsilon})\in[0,\rho_{1}]

definitely for
\varepsilon
small. This concludes the proof.

In the next proposition we see that solutions of
J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u)=0
are, under suitable assumptions, also solutions of
I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u)=0
.

Corollary 5.3.9
.

Let
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
be a sequence of critical points of
J_{\varepsilon}
, that is
J_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})=0
,
satisfying

u_{\varepsilon}\in S(r_{2}^{\prime}),\quad J_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\leq l_{0}^{\prime}\quad\textit{and}\quad\varepsilon\Upsilon(u_{\varepsilon})\in\Omega[0,\nu_{0}]

for any
\varepsilon>0
.
Then, for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, we have

Q_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=0,\quad\textit{and}\quad Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=0.

In particular
I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=0
,
which means that
u_{\varepsilon}
is a solution of (
5.3.21
).

Proof.

By the proof of Lemma
5.3.6
, we notice, since
1-\varphi_{i_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}\equiv 1
outside
\Omega_{i_{\varepsilon}+1}^{\varepsilon}
, and thus outside
\Omega_{h_{0}}/{\varepsilon}
, that

\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}=\|u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}\leq\|u_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}

(5.3.55)

and hence
\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}\to 0
by (
5.3.42
).

Through a careful analysis of the Steps 3–5 of the proof, that is by (
5.3.41
) and (
5.3.55
), we see, more precisely, that

\|u_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}}+\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}^{2s}}+o(1)

where
C=C(r_{2}^{\prime})
and
o(1)
depends on the rate of convergence of
J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})
. Thus, since we assume
J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\equiv 0
, we gain uniformity, i.e., called
\alpha^{*}:=\min\{1,2s\}
, we obtain

\|u_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}\leq\frac{C}{n_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha^{*}}}\sim\varepsilon^{\alpha^{*}/2}

(5.3.56)

As a consequence

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\alpha}}\|u_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus(\Omega_{h_{0}}/\varepsilon))}\to 0\quad\hbox{ as $\varepsilon\to 0$}

for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha^{*}/2)
, and hence
Q_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\equiv Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\equiv 0
for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small.

We want to show now a (truncated) Palais-Smale-like condition.

Proposition 5.3.10
.

There exists
r_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in(0,\min\{r_{0},r_{1}\})
sufficiently small with the following property: let
\varepsilon>0
fixed and let
(u_{j})_{j}\subset S(r_{2}^{\prime\prime})
be such that

\|J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})\|_{(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))^{*}}\to 0\quad\textit{ as $j\to+\infty$}

(5.3.57)

with the additional assumption

(\varepsilon\Upsilon(u_{j}))_{j}\subset\Omega[0,\nu_{0}].

Then
(u_{j})_{j}
admits a strongly convergent subsequence in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

Proof.

Let
r_{2}^{\prime\prime}
to be fixed. Since
S(r_{2}^{\prime\prime})
is bounded, up to a subsequence we can assume
u_{j}\rightharpoonup u_{0}
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. We want to show that

\lim_{R\to+\infty}\lim_{j\to+\infty}\|u_{j}\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R})}=0

for
q=2
and
q=p+1
and conclude by Lemma
5.3.4
.

Arguing similarly to Step 1 of the proof of Lemma
5.3.6
, i.e. exploiting Remark
5.3.8
, we obtain for
L\gg 0
, uniformly in
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|u_{j}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{L}}\leq Cr_{2}^{\prime\prime};

indeed we work with the set
B_{L}-\Upsilon(u_{j})
which expands to
\mathbb{R}^{N}
as
L,j\to+\infty
, since
\Upsilon(u_{j})\in\Omega/\varepsilon
, a fixed bounded set.
Moreover, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|u_{j}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{L+ni}\setminus B_{L+n(i-1)})}\leq(Cr_{2}^{\prime\prime})^{2}

and similarly for the Gagliardo seminorm and the
(p+1)
-norm, thus for some
i_{j,n}\in\{1,\dots,n\}

\|u_{j}\|^{2}_{A^{j,n}}+\|u_{j}\|^{p+1}_{L^{p+1}(A^{j,n})}\leq\frac{C}{n}

where
A^{j,n}:=B_{L+ni_{j,n}}\setminus B_{L+n(i_{j,n}-1)}
. Again similarly to Step 2 of the proof of Lemma
5.3.6
, we introduce
\psi_{j,n}
such that

B_{L+n(i_{j,n}-1)}\prec\psi_{j,n}\prec B_{L+ni_{j,n}}

and
\|\nabla\psi_{j,n}\|_{\infty}=o(1)
as
n\to+\infty
;
moreover we set

\tilde{u}_{j,n}:=(1-\psi_{j,n})u_{j}.

Observe that
\chi_{B_{L}}\leq\psi_{j,n}
, thus
\mathop{\rm supp}(\tilde{u}_{j,n})\subset\complement(B_{L})
.
Arguing as in Step 5 and 3 of the proof of Lemma
5.3.6
we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2N}}\frac{|\tilde{u}_{j,n}(x)-\tilde{u}_{j,n}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}\leq 4\int_{\complement(B_{L})\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|\psi_{j,n}(x)-\psi_{j,n}(y)|^{2}|u_{j}(x)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy+

\displaystyle+4\int_{\complement(B_{L})\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{|u_{j}(x)-u_{j}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{N+2s}}dx\,dy\leq o(1)\|u_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\complement(B_{L}))}^{2}+C[u_{j}]_{\complement(B_{L}),\mathbb{R}^{N}}^{2}

thus
\|\tilde{u}_{j,n}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq Cr_{2}^{\prime\prime}
and hence, choosing
r_{2}^{\prime\prime}
sufficiently small, we have

\|\tilde{u}_{j,n}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq r_{1};

by Lemma
5.3.2
, for
q\in\{2,p+1\}
, we obtain

\|u_{j}\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{L+ni_{j,n}})}^{2}=\|\tilde{u}_{j,n}\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{L+ni_{j,n}})}^{2}\leq C\|\tilde{u}_{j,n}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2}\leq CI^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{u}_{j,n})\tilde{u}_{j,n}.

Thus the claim comes if we show that

I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{u}_{j,n})\tilde{u}_{j,n}\to 0\quad\hbox{ as $j,n\to+\infty$}.

Indeed we have

\displaystyle I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{u}_{j,n})\tilde{u}_{j,n}=I^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})\tilde{u}_{j,n}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2N}}(-\Delta)^{s/2}(\psi_{j,n}u_{j})(-\Delta)^{s/2}((1-\psi_{j,n})u_{j})dx-

\displaystyle-\int_{A^{j,n}}V(\varepsilon x)\psi_{j,n}(1-\psi_{j,n})u_{j}^{2}dx-\int_{A^{j,n}}(f((1-\psi_{j,n})u_{j})-f(u_{j}))(1-\psi_{j,n})u_{j}dx

\displaystyle=:J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})\tilde{u}_{j,n}-Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})\tilde{u}_{j,n}+(I)\leq o(1)+(I)

where we have used that
J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})\to 0
(as
j\to+\infty
, uniformly in
n\in\mathbb{N}
), the boundedness of
\|\tilde{u}_{j,n}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
and the positivity of
Q^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j})\tilde{u}_{j,n}
. The term
(I)
can be estimated in the same way as done in Steps 3-4 of the proof of Lemma
5.3.6
(fixed
j\in\mathbb{N}
, and
n\to+\infty
), and hence we reach the claim.

5.3.3

Deformation lemma on a neighborhood of expected solutions

We want to define now a neighborhood of expected solutions (see
[
119
]
), which will be invariant under a suitable deformation flow.
Consider
r_{3}:=\min\{r^{\prime},r_{0}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime\prime}\}
(see Lemma
5.2.6
, Lemma
5.3.1
, Theorem
5.3.7
and Proposition
5.3.10
), and let us define

R(\delta,u):=\delta-\frac{\delta_{2}}{2}(\widehat{\rho}(u)-\rho_{1})_{+}\leq\delta

and

\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}:=\Big\{u\in S(\rho_{0})\mid\varepsilon\Upsilon(u)\in\Omega[0,\nu_{0}),\;J_{\varepsilon}(u)<E_{m_{0}}+R(\delta,u)\Big\}

where

0<\rho_{1}<\rho_{0}<r_{3},

\varepsilon
is sufficiently small and

\delta\in\left(0,\min\left\{\tfrac{\delta_{2}}{4}(\rho_{0}-\rho_{1}),\,\delta_{1},\,l_{0}^{\prime}-E_{m_{0}}\right\}\right);

(5.3.58)

here
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
are the ones that appear in Lemma
5.3.1
and Theorem
5.3.7
. Notice that the
height
of the sublevel in
\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
depends on
u
itself; this will be used to gain a deformation which preserves
\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
.

We begin by pointing out some geometrical features of the neighborhood
\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
.

•

\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
is open. Indeed,
S(\rho)
and
\{J_{\varepsilon}(u)<E_{m_{0}}+R(\delta,u)\}
are open, and
\Omega[0,\nu_{0})=\Omega(-\gamma,\nu_{0})
for a whatever
\gamma>0
(since
V
cannot go under
m_{0}
in
\Omega
) and thus open. Moreover it is nonempty (see e.g. Section
5.3.4
).

•

If
v\in\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}\subset S(\rho_{0})
, then by (
5.2.17
) we have

\widehat{\rho}(v)<\rho_{0}.

•

If
v\in\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}\subset\{\varepsilon\Upsilon(v)\in\Omega[0,\nu_{0}]\}
, then

\varepsilon\Upsilon(v)\in\Omega[0,\nu_{1}).

(5.3.59)

Indeed, if not, i.e.
\varepsilon\Upsilon(v)\in\Omega[\nu_{1},\nu_{0}]
, then by Lemma
5.3.1
we have

J_{\varepsilon}(v)\geq I_{\varepsilon}(v)\geq E_{m_{0}}+\delta_{1}>E_{m_{0}}+\delta\geq E_{m_{0}}+R(\delta,v)

which is an absurd.

•

If
R(\delta,v)\geq-\delta
then

v\in S(\rho_{0}).

(5.3.60)

Indeed
\frac{\delta_{2}}{4}(\widehat{\rho}(u)-\rho_{1})_{+}\leq\delta
implies, by the restriction on
\delta
,

(\widehat{\rho}(u)-\rho_{1})_{+}<\rho_{0}-\rho_{1}.

If
\widehat{\rho}(u)<\rho_{1}
then clearly
u\in S(\rho_{1})\subset S(\rho_{0})
. If instead
\widehat{\rho}(u)\geq\rho_{1}
, then

\widehat{\rho}(u)<\rho_{0}
,
which again implies
u\in S(\rho_{0}).

We further define the set of critical points of
J_{\varepsilon}
lying in the neighborhood of expected solutions

K_{c}:=\left\{u\in\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}\mid J^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(u)=0,\;J_{\varepsilon}(u)=c\right\},

the sublevel

\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{c}:=\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}\cap J_{\varepsilon}^{c}

and the strip level

(\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{c}_{d}:=\{u\in\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}\mid d\leq J_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq c\},

for every
c,d\in\mathbb{R}
.
We present now a deformation lemma with respect to
K_{c}
, for
c
sufficiently close to
E_{m_{0}}
.

Lemma 5.3.11
.

Let
c\in(E_{m_{0}}-\delta,E_{m_{0}}+\delta)
.
Then there exists a deformation at level
c
, which leaves the set
\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
invariant.
That is, for every
U
neighborhood of
K_{c}
(
U=\emptyset
if
K_{c}=\emptyset
), there exist a small
\omega>0
and a continuous deformation
\eta:[0,1]\times\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}\to\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
such that

(i)

\eta(0,\cdot)=id
;
